# ROUND 2/10

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

### Understanding the Mission
- Score decreases by 1 for items from one source half (GOOD half), increases by 1 for items from the other (BAD half).
- We need robots to identify which half is good and collect from it repeatedly.
- The BLUE vs GREEN walls indicate which half is which. We don't know a priori which is good - we need to explore both.
- Nest has WHITE floor, source has BLACK floor, middle is GRAY. Nest-side wall is RED.

### Key Perceptions Available
- Floor color: BLACK (source), GRAY (middle), WHITE (nest)
- Camera colors: RED (nest wall), BLUE or GREEN (source half walls)
- Neighbors count

### Target Behavior
The robots need to:
1. Go to source (navigate from nest to source)
2. Pick up item (just being there gives an item)
3. Return to nest (deliver item)
4. Repeat

Since we don't know which color (BLUE or GREEN) is the good one, we should try both strategies. The key insight: robots should try to pick from one specific color side and return to nest.

### FSM Design Approaches

**Basic 2-state foraging:**
- State 0: Exploration/GoToColor(BLACK) - go to source
- State 1: GoToColor(WHITE) or GoAwayColor(RED-wall?) - return to nest
- Transition: BLACK floor → switch to return; WHITE floor → switch to collect

**3-state with color discrimination:**
- State 0: GoToColor(BLACK) - approach source
- State 1: GoToColor(BLUE or GREEN) - target specific half
- State 2: GoToColor(RED) - return to nest (RED wall is on nest side)
- Transitions based on floor color

Let me design 14 machines with variety:

**Key color codes:**
- clr=1: Red (nest wall)
- clr=2: Green (one source half)
- clr=3: Blue (other source half)
- clr=4: Yellow
- clr=5: Magenta
- clr=6: Cyan

**Strategy A:** GoToColor(BLUE wall=3) when in middle, return via GoToColor(RED=1) when on BLACK floor
**Strategy B:** GoToColor(GREEN wall=2) when in middle, return via GoToColor(RED=1) when on BLACK floor
**Strategy C:** Exploration to spread out, then directed movement

FSMs:

1. **Elitism/Best guess - 2 state BLUE foraging**: Explore→BLACK floor→GoToColor(RED); WHITE floor→GoToColor(BLUE)
2. **2 state GREEN foraging**
3. **3 state BLUE**: Explore, GoToBlue, ReturnRed
4. **3 state GREEN**: Explore, GoToGreen, ReturnRed
5-14: Variations

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I need to see the results from Round 2 to know which machines performed best. Since no scores were provided, I'll reason from first principles and the designs I submitted.

## Reasoning

### Analysis of Round 2 designs
Without explicit scores, I'll reason about which designs are most likely to work:

The key insight: robots need to go to the BLACK floor (source), pick up an item from ONE specific color side (BLUE or GREEN wall), then return to WHITE floor (nest). The RED wall marks the nest side.

**Most promising designs:**
- 2-state: GoToColor(BLUE/GREEN) → on BLACK floor → GoToColor(RED to return) → on WHITE → repeat
- The simplest directed foraging: GoToColor(3=BLUE) when not at source, GoToColor(1=RED) when at source

**Issues with my Round 2 designs:**
- Some had invalid duplicate parameter indices (e.g., `--l2x0` and `--p2x0` appeared twice)
- Need cleaner, more systematic coverage

### Strategy for Round 3
1. Keep the cleanest 2-state and 3-state designs targeting BLUE (clr=3) and GREEN (clr=2)
2. Add designs that use floor-color transitions more robustly
3. Try GoAwayColor(RED) to push toward source
4. Try 3-state with explicit exploration phase

The core loop: **GoToColor(target_half) → BLACK floor detected → GoToColor(RED nest wall) → WHITE floor detected → repeat**

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I notice I haven't been given actual scores from the previous rounds. I'll reason carefully from first principles about what should work best.

## Reasoning

### Mission Analysis
- Score DECREASES (good) when delivering items from one half, INCREASES (bad) from the other
- We don't know which color (BLUE=3 or GREEN=2) is the good one - need to try both
- Navigation: BLACK=source, WHITE=nest, GRAY=middle, RED wall=nest side

### Core Behavioral Loop
The most reliable foraging loop:
1. **Approach source**: GoToColor(BLUE or GREEN wall) while on GRAY/WHITE
2. **At source** (BLACK floor detected): switch to return
3. **Return to nest**: GoToColor(RED wall) while on GRAY/BLACK  
4. **At nest** (WHITE floor detected): switch back to approach

### Key Insight
The simplest 2-state machines should be most robust:
- State 0: GoToColor(target_color) → transition on BLACK floor → State 1
- State 1: GoToColor(RED=1) → transition on WHITE floor → State 0

Using probability 1.0 for floor transitions makes them deterministic and reliable.

### Variants to try
- Both BLUE and GREEN targeting
- Different transition probabilities (0.8, 0.9, 0.95, 1.0)
- 3-state with exploration buffer
- Using GoAwayColor(RED) to push toward source as alternative

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I still haven't received actual scores. Let me reason more carefully about what could be going wrong and diversify my approach significantly.

## Reasoning

### What might be failing
The basic 2-state GoToColor(BLUE/GREEN) → GoToColor(RED) loop seems theoretically sound, but perhaps:
1. Robots can't reliably see colored walls from far away - they may need to be closer
2. The floor-color transitions (BLACK→return, WHITE→collect) might conflict if transitions fire too eagerly
3. GoAwayColor(RED) might work better for pushing toward the source than GoToColor(BLUE/GREEN)

### Alternative approaches to try
- **Exploration-based**: Use random walk to source area, rely on BLACK floor detection, then GoToColor(RED) to return
- **GoAwayColor(RED)**: Move away from red wall = move toward source. When BLACK detected → GoToColor(RED)
- **Neighbor-based**: Use attraction/repulsion with color triggers
- **Probabilistic transitions**: Add random escape transitions to avoid getting stuck

### Key reconsideration
Maybe I should try `GoAwayColor(RED=1)` as the "go to source" behavior - moving away from the nest wall naturally heads toward the source. Combined with floor detection, this could be more reliable than trying to spot BLUE/GREEN walls from a distance.

Also trying pure exploration with GoToColor(RED) return - robots naturally end up at source via random walk, then return deterministically.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I still haven't received actual scores. Let me fundamentally reconsider my approach.

## Reasoning

### Critical Rethink

I've been assuming GoToColor works well, but I need to think about what robots actually perceive. The camera sees colored objects (walls, blocks). The floor sensor detects floor color.

**Key realization**: The scoring says one half DECREASES score (good) and the other INCREASES score (bad). We don't know which is BLUE or GREEN. So I need FSMs that work regardless of which color is good - OR I need to try both and see which scores lower.

**Another key insight**: Maybe the problem is that robots starting in the center can't see the colored walls at all initially. They need to navigate to the source area first.

**Simplest working loop I haven't fully tried**:
- GoAwayColor(RED) naturally pushes robots toward the source (away from nest wall)
- Once on BLACK, GoToColor(RED) returns them
- This is color-agnostic and should work for EITHER half

**But which half do they enter?** Random exploration means they enter both halves roughly equally → net score ≈ 0. That's not helpful.

**To get negative score**: Must preferentially enter ONE half. The only way to do this with available sensors is to use camera to detect BLUE or GREEN walls and navigate toward one specifically.

**Revised strategy**: Try GoToColor(BLUE=3) for source approach. If BLUE is the good side, score goes very negative. If GREEN is good, try that. Need to cover both aggressively.

Also try: robots that go to source randomly but use the wall color to decide whether to stay or leave immediately.

# ROUND 7/10

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I still haven't received actual scores. Let me completely rethink the approach.

## Reasoning

### Fundamental Reconsideration

After 6 rounds without feedback, I need to think about what might be systematically wrong.

**Problem 1: Which color is "good"?**
The mission says one half decreases score (good) and one increases (bad). I've been trying both BLUE and GREEN, but perhaps I should focus on making BOTH variants strong and let the evolutionary process pick.

**Problem 2: Maybe the robots can't see BLUE/GREEN walls until very close**
If the arena is large, GoToColor(BLUE) might not work well from the middle. A two-phase approach might be better: explore until near the source, THEN use color discrimination.

**Problem 3: The return trip**
GoToColor(RED=1) should work for return since the RED wall is on the nest side. But maybe the RED wall isn't visible from deep in the source area.

**Problem 4: What if I'm overthinking this?**
The simplest possible thing: random walk to get items, GoToColor(RED) to return. Items from both halves cancel out → score ≈ 0. NOT useful.

**What actually scores negative:**
Must deliver MORE items from the good half than bad half. The only discriminating sensor is the camera detecting BLUE vs GREEN. So robots MUST use color discrimination.

**Best strategy**: GoToColor(BLUE) to approach → on BLACK floor → GoToColor(RED) to return. This should work IF blue is the good side. Pair with GREEN version.

**New idea**: Use a 3-state machine where state 0 is GoToColor(BLUE), state 1 detects being on BLACK and briefly uses GoToColor(BLUE) to stay in BLUE half, then transitions to GoToColor(RED).

Actually the key issue: when robot enters BLACK floor from BLUE side, it already has a BLUE item. It should immediately return. The 1-transition version (BLACK→return) is correct.

Let me try much more varied topologies and also try clr=4,5,6 (yellow, magenta, cyan) in case those map to relevant colors.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
I still haven't received actual scores. This is very concerning - I may be generating invalid FSMs that crash the simulator, or all scores are near 0. Let me do a thorough validity check and rethink.

## Reasoning

### Validity Audit
Let me check my FSMs carefully for format errors:
- `--n<I>` = number of transitions from state I
- `--n<I>x<K>` = destination of transition K from state I (indexed among OTHER states)
- With 2 states, from state 0: only other state is 1, so D must be 0 (meaning state 1)
- With 2 states, from state 1: only other state is 0, so D must be 0 (meaning state 0)
- With 3 states, from state 0: others are [1,2], D=0→state1, D=1→state2
- With 3 states, from state 1: others are [0,2], D=0→state0, D=1→state2
- With 3 states, from state 2: others are [0,1], D=0→state0, D=1→state1

**I think my destination indices may have been wrong in some cases!** Let me be very careful.

### Correct Destination Encoding
For 3 states, leaving state 1: the "others" are states 0 and 2. So:
- D=0 → goes to state 0
- D=1 → goes to state 2

For 3 states, leaving state 2: the "others" are states 0 and 1. So:
- D=0 → goes to state 0  
- D=1 → goes to state 1

### Carefully Constructed FSMs

**2-state BLUE forager** (verified):
- State 0: GoToColor(BLUE=3), transition on BLACK floor (C=0) → state 1 (D=0)
- State 1: GoToColor(RED=1), transition on WHITE floor (C=2) → state 0 (D=0)

**3-state with exploration** (verified):
- State 0: Explore, BLACK→state1(D=0), WHITE→state2(D=1)
- State 1: GoToColor(BLUE=3), BLACK→state2(D=1)  [wait, from state1, D=1→state2]
- State 2: GoToColor(RED=1), WHITE→state0(D=0), GRAY→state1(D=1) [from state2, D=0→state0, D=1→state1]

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
Still no scores provided. Let me do a complete systematic re-examination.

## Reasoning

### Root Cause Analysis

After 8 rounds with no feedback, I suspect my FSMs may be systematically crashing due to a format error I keep repeating. Let me re-read the spec very carefully.

**Critical rule re-read:**
- `--n<I>x<K> D`: destination D is indexed among the OTHER states only
- With 2 states, from state 0: others = [state1], so D=0 means state1
- With 2 states, from state 1: others = [state0], so D=0 means state0
- With 3 states, from state 0: others = [state1, state2], D=0→state1, D=1→state2
- With 3 states, from state 1: others = [state0, state2], D=0→state0, D=1→state2
- With 3 states, from state 2: others = [state0, state1], D=0→state0, D=1→state1

This looks correct in my previous FSMs for 2-state and 3-state cases.

**For 4 states, from state 1:** others = [state0, state2, state3], D=0→state0, D=1→state2, D=2→state3
**For 4 states, from state 2:** others = [state0, state1, state3], D=0→state0, D=1→state1, D=2→state3

I may have had errors in my 4-state FSMs! Let me focus on clean 2-state and 3-state machines only this round.

**Also: LED color constraint**: `--cle<I>` must be one of {0,4,5,6} — NOT 1,2,3! I've been using 4,5,6,0 which are valid. Good.

**Behavior parameter check:**
- B=8 GoToColor: needs `--vel<I> 1.0` and `--clr<I>`
- B=9 GoAwayColor: needs `--vel<I> 1.0` and `--clr<I>`
- B=0 Exploration: needs `--rwm<I>`

**Condition parameter check:**
- C=7 (perceives color): needs `--l<I>x<K>` AND `--p<I>x<K>`
- C=0,1,2 (floor): needs `--p<I>x<K>`
- C=5 (random): needs `--p<I>x<K>`

Everything looks syntactically correct. The issue might be semantic - the FSMs just don't score well (score near 0 because they equally collect from both halves, or the simulator gives 0 for broken behaviors).

**New hypothesis**: Maybe BLUE=3 is the BAD side and GREEN=2 is GOOD. Or vice versa. Since I don't know, I must cover both. But also - maybe scores ARE being recorded and both sides are being tried, with the better one selected. Let me just make very clean, correct FSMs.

Let me also try: what if I use `--clr=4` (yellow) for GoToColor? Yellow might correspond to something. Actually no - colors 1-6 map to {red, green, blue, yellow, magenta, cyan} for the camera targets, detecting wall/block colors. The walls are RED, BLUE, GREEN. So clr=1(red), clr=2(green), clr=3(blue) are the relevant ones.

Let me make 13 very clean, verified FSMs - 2-state and 3-state only:

## Reasoning

### Final Round Analysis

After 9 rounds without score feedback, I need to make my best possible guesses for this final round. The FSMs are syntactically correct (I've verified the format carefully), so they should be running. The lack of feedback may simply mean the conversation format doesn't show scores.

### What I know theoretically works:
The core foraging loop:
1. GoToColor(target_color) → navigate to source area
2. On BLACK floor → switch to return
3. GoToColor(RED=1) → navigate to nest  
4. On WHITE floor → switch back to approach

The question is whether BLUE(3) or GREEN(2) is the good side. I need to cover both.

### Final strategy - maximize coverage of both variants with clean verified FSMs:

**Group A (BLUE=3 targeting)**: 4 FSMs
**Group B (GREEN=2 targeting)**: 4 FSMs  
**Group C (GoAwayColor(RED) for source approach)**: 2 FSMs
**Group D (Exploration-based)**: 2 FSMs

All using deterministic (p=1.0) floor transitions for reliability. Keeping to 2-state and 3-state only to avoid destination-index errors in 4-state machines.

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

The arena contains a nest area at one end, with a WHITE floor, and a source area at the opposite end, with a BLACK floor. The floor in between is GRAY. The wall on the nest side is RED.

The source area is divided into two halves. The wall blocks behind one half are BLUE and those behind the other half are GREEN.

A robot carries at most one item. Entering a half of the source gives the robot an item of that half's type, replacing any item it was already carrying. Entering the nest with an item delivers it: an item from one half decreases the score by 1, an item from the other half increases it by 1.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
